stage=initial_retrievalrank=6query_text=For v/c ≪ 1 and ζ ≲ 1, what asymptotic form is given for Ξ0(y, τ)?node_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4:text:10:part:0point_id=cc95f23e-2c9b-5216-b908-4179dfca59a4doc_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4source=data\demo_docs\2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effect.pdftitle=2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effectpage=2chunk_index=10modality=textchannel=vectorvector_name=textparser_name=minerusource_parser=mineruscore=0.4868score_vector=0.5310score_bm25=nullscore_rrf=0.0161rerank_score=nullscore_composite=0.4868score_policy=weighted_v1threshold_passed=Trueexpanded_from=nullexpansion_relation=nullcandidate_pool=nullexpansion_mode=nullexpansion_allowed_by=nullseed_rank=nullseed_threshold=nullrelation_priority=nullreplaced_relation=nullretry_carry_forward=nullretry_carry_forward_added=nullretry_carry_forward_matched=nullretry_carry_forward_replaced_current=nullretry_carry_forward_replaced_current_score=nullretry_carry_forward_from_stage=nullretry_carry_forward_previous_rank=nullretry_carry_forward_score=nullagent_relevance_score=nullagent_relevance_label=nullagent_relevance_source=nullagent_relevance_cache_hit=nullagent_grade_cache_key=nullagent_relevance_keep=nullagent_relevance_drop=nullagent_relevance_drop_cached=nullagent_label_score_delta=nullagent_related_modality_delta=nullagent_related_context_text_delta=nullagent_related_context_node_id=nullagent_related_context_present=nullagent_related_context_added=nullagent_related_context_fixed_score=nullagent_relevance_reasoning=null
rerank_score=null
\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq.

stage=initial_retrievalrank=11query_text=For v/c ≪ 1 and ζ ≲ 1, what asymptotic form is given for Ξ0(y, τ)?node_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4:text:11:part:0point_id=57af5b85-82fc-51ec-963d-bf959542ea2ddoc_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4source=data\demo_docs\2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effect.pdftitle=2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effectpage=3chunk_index=11modality=textchannel=vectorvector_name=textparser_name=minerusource_parser=mineruscore=0.4100score_vector=0.4556score_bm25=nullscore_rrf=0.0145rerank_score=nullscore_composite=0.4100score_policy=weighted_v1threshold_passed=Trueexpanded_from=nullexpansion_relation=nullcandidate_pool=nullexpansion_mode=nullexpansion_allowed_by=nullseed_rank=nullseed_threshold=nullrelation_priority=nullreplaced_relation=nullretry_carry_forward=nullretry_carry_forward_added=nullretry_carry_forward_matched=nullretry_carry_forward_replaced_current=nullretry_carry_forward_replaced_current_score=nullretry_carry_forward_from_stage=nullretry_carry_forward_previous_rank=nullretry_carry_forward_score=nullagent_relevance_score=nullagent_relevance_label=nullagent_relevance_source=nullagent_relevance_cache_hit=nullagent_grade_cache_key=nullagent_relevance_keep=nullagent_relevance_drop=nullagent_relevance_drop_cached=nullagent_label_score_delta=nullagent_related_modality_delta=nullagent_related_context_text_delta=nullagent_related_context_node_id=nullagent_related_context_present=nullagent_related_context_added=nullagent_related_context_fixed_score=nullagent_relevance_reasoning=null
rerank_score=null
\tag {17}
$$

For $\vartheta \ll 1$ ( $\theta \ll \theta_0$ ), we have

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y} \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta . \tag {18}
$$

The asymptotics (17) and (18) for the cross sections are independent of $s = \operatorname{sgn}(q)$ , since either the function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ contributes to the cross section for $\vartheta \gg 1$ , or $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ for $\vartheta \ll 1$ . However, for $\vartheta \sim 1$ an asymmetry arises due to the interference of the functions $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ . This asymmetry will be an alternating function of $\tau$ and can be observed by measuring the non-stationary Hall effect (oscillating voltage in the transverse direction).

Let us represent the cross section $d\sigma(\vartheta, \tau)$ in (16) in the form

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = d \sigma_ {s} (\vartheta , \tau) + s d \sigma_ {a} (\vartheta , \tau),
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {s} (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left[ G _ {1} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta^ {2}} G _ {2} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right] d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {a} (\theta , \tau) = 2 \sigma_ {0} G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta}. \tag {19}
$$

The functions $G_{1}(\vartheta,\tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta,\tau)$ depend on the parameter Q and variable $\tau$ only through the combination $f = Q\cos\tau$ . Fig. 1 shows the dependence of $\Sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ and $\Sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ on $\vartheta$ for several values of f.

However, for $\\vartheta \\sim 1$ an asymmetry arises due to the interference of the functions $G_{1}(\\vartheta, \\tau)$ and $G_{2}(\\vartheta, \\tau)$ . This asymmetry will be an alternating function of $\\tau$ and can be observed by measuring the non-stationary Hall effect (oscillating voltage in the transverse direction).\n\nLet us represent the cross section $d\\sigma(\\vartheta, \\tau)$ in (16) in the form\n\n$$\nd \\sigma (\\vartheta , \\tau) = d \\sigma_ {s} (\\vartheta , \\tau) + s d \\sigma_ {a} (\\vartheta , \\tau),\n$$\n\n$$\nd \\sigma_ {s} (\\theta , \\tau) = \\sigma_ {0} \\left[ G _ {1} ^ {2} (\\vartheta , \\tau) + \\frac {1}{\\vartheta^ {2}} G _ {2} ^ {2} (\\vartheta , \\tau) \\right] d \\vartheta ,\n$$\n\n$$\nd \\sigma_ {a} (\\theta , \\tau) = 2 \\sigma_ {0} G _ {1} (\\vartheta , \\tau) G _ {2} (\\vartheta , \\tau) \\frac {d \\vartheta}{\\vartheta}. \\tag {19}\n$$\n\nThe functions $G_{1}(\\vartheta,\\tau)$ and $G_{2}(\\vartheta,\\tau)$ depend on the parameter Q and variable $\\tau$ only through the combination $f = Q\\cos\\tau$ . stage=initial_expandedrank=6query_text=For v/c ≪ 1 and ζ ≲ 1, what asymptotic form is given for Ξ0(y, τ)?node_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4:text:10:part:0point_id=cc95f23e-2c9b-5216-b908-4179dfca59a4doc_id=bad4486a9b70f8e94b4a5b409d3ffdb4source=data\demo_docs\2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effect.pdftitle=2412.16030_Non-stationary_Aharonov-Bohm_effectpage=2chunk_index=10modality=textchannel=vectorvector_name=textparser_name=minerusource_parser=mineruscore=0.4868score_vector=0.5310score_bm25=nullscore_rrf=0.0161rerank_score=nullscore_composite=0.4868score_policy=weighted_v1threshold_passed=Trueexpanded_from=nullexpansion_relation=nullcandidate_pool=nullexpansion_mode=nullexpansion_allowed_by=nullseed_rank=nullseed_threshold=nullrelation_priority=nullreplaced_relation=nullretry_carry_forward=nullretry_carry_forward_added=nullretry_carry_forward_matched=nullretry_carry_forward_replaced_current=nullretry_carry_forward_replaced_current_score=nullretry_carry_forward_from_stage=nullretry_carry_forward_previous_rank=nullretry_carry_forward_score=nullagent_relevance_score=nullagent_relevance_label=nullagent_relevance_source=nullagent_relevance_cache_hit=nullagent_grade_cache_key=nullagent_relevance_keep=nullagent_relevance_drop=nullagent_relevance_drop_cached=nullagent_label_score_delta=nullagent_related_modality_delta=nullagent_related_context_text_delta=nullagent_related_context_node_id=nullagent_related_context_present=nullagent_related_context_added=nullagent_related_context_fixed_score=nullagent_relevance_reasoning=null

\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq.

rerank_score=null
\tag {17}
$$

For $\vartheta \ll 1$ ( $\theta \ll \theta_0$ ), we have

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| \int_ {0} ^ {Q | \cos \tau |} (1 - \cos y) \frac {d y}{y} \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta . \tag {18}
$$

The asymptotics (17) and (18) for the cross sections are independent of $s = \operatorname{sgn}(q)$ , since either the function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ contributes to the cross section for $\vartheta \gg 1$ , or $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ for $\vartheta \ll 1$ . However, for $\vartheta \sim 1$ an asymmetry arises due to the interference of the functions $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ . This asymmetry will be an alternating function of $\tau$ and can be observed by measuring the non-stationary Hall effect (oscillating voltage in the transverse direction).

Let us represent the cross section $d\sigma(\vartheta, \tau)$ in (16) in the form

$$
d \sigma (\vartheta , \tau) = d \sigma_ {s} (\vartheta , \tau) + s d \sigma_ {a} (\vartheta , \tau),
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {s} (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left[ G _ {1} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta^ {2}} G _ {2} ^ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right] d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
d \sigma_ {a} (\theta , \tau) = 2 \sigma_ {0} G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \frac {d \vartheta}{\vartheta}. \tag {19}
$$

The functions $G_{1}(\vartheta,\tau)$ and $G_{2}(\vartheta,\tau)$ depend on the parameter Q and variable $\tau$ only through the combination $f = Q\cos\tau$ . Fig. 1 shows the dependence of $\Sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{s}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ and $\Sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau) = \sigma_{0}^{-1}d\sigma_{a}(\vartheta,\tau)/d\vartheta$ on $\vartheta$ for several values of f.

# III. CONCLUSION

The non-stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect is investigated in the non-relativistic approximation. Using the eikonal approach, the differential and total scattering cross sections are found. In contrast to the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect, the total cross section in the non-stationary case is finite. The main contribution to the scattering cross section comes from the angles $\theta \sim \theta_{0} = \hbar\omega/E \ll 1$ . The differential scattering cross section contains an asymmetry with respect to the replacement $q \rightarrow -q$ . This asymmetry has a maximum at $\theta \sim \theta_{0}$ and decreases rapidly at $\theta \gg \theta_{0}$ and $\theta \ll \theta_{0}$ . The asymmetry in the cross section can be observed as the non-stationary Hall effect, and the total scattering cross section manifests itself in oscillations of the total electron flux.

\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq.

\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq.

\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq.

\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq.

eft[ J _ {1} (\zeta r) \sin (\phi) - N _ {1} (\zeta r) \cos (\phi) \right],

\tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result. To do this, we set $\omega \to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]

$$
d \sigma = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P} \sin^ {2} Q \frac {d \theta}{\theta^ {2}}. \tag {14}
$$

For $v/c \ll 1$ and $\zeta \lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \ll 1$ and $z \ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\Xi_{0}(y, \tau)$ of the function $\Xi(y, \tau)$ ,

$$
\Xi_ {0} (y, \tau) = Q \operatorname{sgn} (y) \cos \tau \exp (- | y | / \vartheta),
$$

$$
\vartheta = \frac {v}{c \zeta} = \frac {\theta}{\theta_ {0}}, \quad \theta_ {0} = \frac {\hbar \omega}{2 E}. \tag {15}
$$

Using integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form

$$
d \sigma (\theta , \tau) = \sigma_ {0} \left| s G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) + \frac {1}{\vartheta} G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) \right| ^ {2} d \vartheta ,
$$

$$
G _ {1} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
G _ {2} (\vartheta , \tau) = \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} d y \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) \sin [ \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) ] \sin (\vartheta y),
$$

$$
\sigma_ {0} = \frac {2 \hbar}{\pi P \theta_ {0}} = \frac {2 v}{\pi \omega}, \quad \Xi_ {1} (y, \tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \cos \tau . \tag {16}
$$

The function $G_{1}(\vartheta, \tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\cos \tau \to -\cos \tau$ , while $G_{2}(\vartheta, \tau)$ does not change sign. For convenience, in Eq.

To do this, we set $\\omega \\to 0$ before the change of variables, and obtain the differential scattering cross section for the stationary Aharonov-Bohm effect [1, 11]\n\n$$\nd \\sigma = \\frac {2 \\hbar}{\\pi P} \\sin^ {2} Q \\frac {d \\theta}{\\theta^ {2}}. \\tag {14}\n$$\n\nFor $v/c \\ll 1$ and $\\zeta \\lesssim 1$ the main contribution to the cross section is determined by the integration region $y \\ll 1$ and $z \\ll 1$ . In this case we find the asymptotics $\\Xi_{0}(y, \\tau)$ of the function $\\Xi(y, \\tau)$ ,\n\n$$\n\\Xi_ {0} (y, \\tau) = Q \\operatorname{sgn} (y) \\cos \\tau \\exp (- | y | / \\vartheta),\n$$\n\n$$\n\\vartheta = \\frac {v}{c \\zeta} = \\frac {\\theta}{\\theta_ {0}}, \\quad \\theta_ {0} = \\frac {\\hbar \\omega}{2 E}. \\tag {15}\n$$\n\nUsing integration by parts and the asymptotics (15), we write the expression for the cross section in the form\n\n$$\nd \\sigma (\\theta , \\tau) = \\sigma_ {0} \\left| s G _ {1} (\\vartheta , \\tau) + \\frac {1}{\\vartheta} G _ {2} (\\vartheta , \\tau) \\right| ^ {2} d \\vartheta ,\n$$\n\n$$\nG _ {1} (\\vartheta , \\tau) = \\int_ {0} ^ {\\infty} d y \\sin [ \\Xi_ {1} (y, \\tau) ] \\sin (\\vartheta y),\n$$\n\n$$\nG _ {2} (\\vartheta , \\tau) = \\int_ {0} ^ {\\infty} d y \\Xi_ {1} (y, \\tau) \\sin [ \\Xi_ {1} (y, \\tau) ] \\sin (\\vartheta y),\n$$\n\n$$\n\\sigma_ {0} = \\frac {2 \\hbar}{\\pi P \\theta_ {0}} = \\frac {2 v}{\\pi \\omega}, \\quad \\Xi_ {1} (y, \\tau) = Q e ^ {- y} \\cos \\tau . \\tag {16}\n$$\n\nThe function $G_{1}(\\vartheta, \\tau)$ changes sign when replacing $\\cos \\tau \\to -\\cos \\tau$ , while $G_{2}(\\vartheta, \\tau)$ does not change sign. eft[ J _ {1} (\zeta r) \sin (\phi) - N _ {1} (\zeta r) \cos (\phi) \right],
$$

$$
\zeta = \frac {k}{| q |} = \frac {\hbar k}{P \theta}, Q = \frac {e \Phi_ {0}}{2 c \hbar}, \phi = \tau + \frac {c}{v} \zeta z,
$$

$$
r = \sqrt {y ^ {2} + z ^ {2}}, \quad s = \operatorname{sgn} q. \tag {13}
$$

Recall that $\tau = \omega(t - x/v)$ and the cross section in the two-dimensional case has the dimension of length. Let us show that in the stationary case the expression (13) goes over to the known result.
